(Office of the Chief Controller of Imports and Exports) 
SUSPENSION ORDER 
New Delhi, the 27th June, 1990 

S.O. 2129.—M/s. OM Impex Private Limited, 7, Pudda 
Piikur Road, Calcutta-20 obtained from the office of the 
CCl&E, New Delhi an Import Licence No. P/F 0351483 
dated 2-3-90 for Rs. 37,41,200 (Thirty Seven lakhs forty one 
thousand & two hundred only) for import of spares for 
stock & Sale under para 79(i) of 1989—91 Import Policy. 

2. It has been observed that the firm Produced forged 
documents by fradulcnt means, and there is no such firm viz. 
M/s. Nand Lnl & Co., Chartered Accountant, Salkia Howrah, 
Calcutta who had Is-ued a Chartered Accountant Certificate 
to the firm. 

3. And whereas, proceedings for cancellation of the said 
licence has been intimated under cl. 9(i) (d) of the Imports 
Control Order, 1955 as amended for the reason that the said 
licence will not serve the purpose for which it was issued and 
also for reasons stated in Para 2 above, 

4. Therefore, in exercise of tlic Powers vested in me under 
cl. 9(3) of the Import (Control) Order 1955, dt. 7-12-55 as 
amended, I hereby suspend the operation of the aforesaid 
licence No. P/F 0351483 dated 2-3-90. 

[F. No. l-0/Spares/AM-98/ALS]136] 

MRS. MAYA D. KEM. Dy. Chief Controller of

(DepaitmcnEof Education) 

New Delhi, the 25th July, 1990 

S.O. 2138.—Whereas by noiificafions of the Government 
of India in the Ministry of Human Resource Development 
(Department of Education) No. S.O, 798 (E), dated the 24th 


1990/W'II 20, 1912 


October. 1985, No. S.O. 773(E) dated the 21st October, 1986 
and S.O. 97(E;, dated the 4th November, 1987 issued in 
pursuance of sub-section (1) of section 3 of the Aurovil.e 
(Emergency Provisions) Act, 1980 (59 of 1980) thereinafter 
referred to as the said Act), the management of Auroville was 
vested in the Central Government for a temporary period, 
On year to year basis, upto 9th November, 1988. 

2. And whereas tlie Administrator, Auroville had requested 
the Central Governnieni that certain disputes relating to 
Auroville be referred to a Tribunal under pub-section (1) of 
section 9 of the said Act; 

3. And whereas in exercise of the powers cOuterved by sub- 
tection (1) of section 9 of the said Act, the Central Govern¬ 
ment appointed Justice S. D. Agarwalu, Judge, Allabhabad 
High Court as one member Tribunal, vide Notification No. 
S.O. 503, date d the 13th January, 1986, and referred the 
said disputes relating to Auroville to the said Tribunal; 

4. Now whereas the disputes referred to the Tribunal 
vide Government of India in the Miininstry of Human Re¬ 
source Development (Department of Educotion) Notification 
Numbers S.O, 503, dated the 13th January, 19896 and Noti¬ 
fication No. 4?.22/83-UU, dated the 22nd September, 1986 
and the 2lHt June, 1988 have been adjudicated upon by the 
said Tribunal and finally decided, the Central Government 
hereby and through this notification wind up the said tribunal 
with immediate effect.

(ARCHAEOLOGY) 

S.O. 2140.—Whereas by a notification oi’ the Government 
ol India in the Department of Culture (Achacological Survey 
of India) No, S.O. 37, dated the 21st December, 1989 pub¬ 
lished in Part-Il, section 3, sub-section (ii) of the Gazette 
of India, dated the 6th January, 1990, the Central Govern¬ 
ment gave two months notice of its intention to declare the 
pre-historic Rock-shelters specified in the Schedule to the 
said notification to be of national importance and a copy 
of the said notification was afflxed at a conspicuous place 
near the said caves as required by sub-section (1) of section 4 
of the Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and 
Remains Act, 1958 (24 of 19.58) 


And whereas the said Gazette was made available to the 
public on the 16th February. 1991). 


And vvhercas no objection from the public has been received 
by the Central Government ; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-section (3) of section 4 of the said Act, the Central 
Government hereby declares the pre-historic Rock-shelters and 
caves specified In the Schedule annexed hereto to be of 
national importance.

S.O, 2141.—Whereas the Central GovcrnmciU is ot opinion 
that the ancient site and archaeological remains specified in 
I he Schedule annexed hereto are of national importance ; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
sub-section (1) of section 4 of the Ancient Monuments and 
Archaeological Sites and Remains Act. 1958 124 of 1958), 


the Central Government hereby give.s notices of its Intention 
to declare the said ancient site and archaeological remains 
to be of national importance. 

Any objection which may be received within a period of 
two months from the date of issue of this notificat'on 
in the Official Gazette from any person interested in the 
.'laid ancient site and archaeological remains will be taken 
into consideration by the central Government.

New Deliii, the 18th July, 1990 

S.O. 2142.—Whereas by the notification of the Government of India in the Ministry of 
Energy (Department of Coal) No, S.O. 472, dated the 16th February, 1989 under sub-section (1) of sec- 
on 7 of the Coal Bearing Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 1957 (20 of 1957) and publisheid in 
Part 11, section 3, sub-section (ii) of the Gazette of India, dated the 11th March, 1989. the Central Govern¬ 
ment gave notice of its intention to acquire the rights to mine, quarry, bore, dig and search for win, work an 
carry away the minerals in the lands measuring 1693.437 acres (approximately), or.685.311 hectares (.ap 
proximately) in the locality specified in the Schedule appended to that notification; 

And whereas the competent autfiority in pursuance of sections of the said Act. has made his 
report the Central Government; 

And whereas the Central Government after considering the report aforesaid and alter constilting the 
Government of Madhya Pradesh is satisfied that the rights to mine, quarry, bore, dig, and search for win 
work and carry away minerals in the lands measuring 685.311 hectares (approximately) or 1(S93.437 acres 
appro;fimateiy) described in the Schedule appended hereto, should be acquired.

S.O. 2143. - Wheiv:is it Appears lo the Central Govcrmncnl that coal i.s likely to be obtained 
from the lands iji the locality mriitioned in the Schedule hereto annexed; 

Now therefore in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 4 of the Coal Bearing 
Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 1957 (20 of 1957), the Central Government heieby gives 
notice of its intention to prospect for coal therein; 

The plan bearing No. C-1 (E)/III/GR/461-390 dated the 1st March, 1990 of the area covered by this 
notification can be inspected at the Office of the Western Coalfields Limited (Revenue Department), Coal 
Estate, Civil Lines, Nagpur-44000] or at the Office of the Collector Chhindwara (Madhya Pradesh) or at 
the Office of the Coal Controller, 1, Council House Street, Calcutta, 

All persons interested in the lands covered by this notification shall deliver all maps, charts and other 
documents referred to in sub-section (7) of section 13 of the said Act to the Revenue Officer, Western Coal¬ 
fields Limited, Coal Estate Civil Lines, Nagpur-440001 within ninety days from the date of publication 
of this notification in the Official Gazette.

New Delhi, the 23rd July, 1990 

S.O. 2144.—^Whereas by the notification of the Government of India in the Ministry 
of Energy (Department of Coal) No. S-O. 3017 dated the 14th September, 1988 issued under sub-section (1) 
of section 4 of the Coal Bearing Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 1957 (20 of 1957) (hereinafter 
referred to as the said Act) and published in Part II, Section 3, sub-section (ii) of the Gazette of India dated 
the 8th October 1988, the Central Government gave notice of its intention to prospect for coal in lands mea¬ 
suring 4944.41 hectares (approximately) or 12218.13 acres (approximately )in the locality specified in the 
Schedule appended thereto as also in the Schedule hereto annexed; 

And whereas in respect of the said lands no notice under sub-section (1) of Section 7 of the said Act 
has been given. 

Now therefore in exercise of the powers conferred by the said sub-section (1). the Central 
Government hereby specifies a further period of one year commencing on the 8th October 1990 
as the period within which the Central Government may give notice of its intention to acquire the said lands 
or any rights in or over such lands.

New Delhi, the 12th July, 1990 

S.O. 2150.—In pursuance of Section 17 of the Indnstri 
Disputes Act 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Govemme 

award of the Central Cover 
ment Industrial Tnbunal-Cum-Labour Court Kanpur as shov 
m the Annexure in the industrial dispute between the emnh 
yerx In relation to the management of State Bank of Ind^ ai 

S rJ2Ti9^o""'^ «-en 


AtVWEXURE 


BEFORE SHRI ARJAN DEV, PRESIDING Opprr’uD 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTOlAT TSm^? 
CUM.I.ABOUE COURT, MNEf! MOAR™Tw 
I ndustrial Dispute No. 106 of 1988 


AWARD 

1. The Central Government. Ministry of Labour vide 
its Notification on No. L-12012/321/8J-O.11 (A) ' dated 
4-8-11988 has referred the following dispute for adjudication 
to this Tribunal : 

Whether the action of the management of State Bank 
of ffidia local Head Office, Lucknow in relation to 
their U.P. Civil Scett, Branch Lucknow in termina¬ 
ting the services of Shri Raju Dutt Pandey Ex- 
Messenger w.e.f. 3-2-82 is justified 7 If not, to what 
relief is the workman concerned entitled 7 

2. The workman’s case in short is that he was appointed 
as Messenger on a daily wage of Rs. 5 on 17-8-78 at Gonda 
Branch of the State Bank of India. He continued working 
in the said branch upto 31-5-79 vide Annc.vure 1 which S 
the copy of certificate dated 19-1-87 issued by the Branch 
Manager Gonda Branch. The certificate is to the effect 
ffiat the workman had worked for 101 days in broken periods 
as temporary messenger. He alleges that he was again en- 
gaged In the said branch On 16-5-80, It is not stated as to 
tui what time after his reemployment he continued working 
te the said branch. According to him be was not paid for 
Sundays and Holidays. 

3. The workman further alleges that on 12-10-81 he was 
appointed in the Civil Seett. Branch, Lucknow of the State 
Bank of India as a full time employee on daily wages and 
worked at the said branch till 2-3-82 vide annexuie II which 
IS the copy pf certificate Issued by the Branch Manager, 
Annexure II is dated 8-2-85, The certificate shows that the 
workman had worked as a messenger on a daily wage of 
Rs, 7 for a period of 88 days. During tlie period 12-10-81 
to 3-2-82. According to him bis services wer’e di.spcilced 
with w.e.f. 4-2-82 in violation of the provi.sions of Section 
25-Q read with rule 77 of 1. D, Cenlral Rules 1957, Accord¬ 
ing to him in ffis vacancy several persons numbering 15 
nhmed by him were allowed to work. The management thus 
violated the provisions of Section 25-H 1, D. Act read with 
Rule 78 of the I. D. (Central) Rules, 1957, He ha.s therefore, 
prayed that the order of termination of his services he set 
aside and his reinstatement be ordered ivith continuity of 
service and back wages. 

4. The maagemenf in their written statemciii plead that 
the workman was initially engaged as a casual worker on a 
daily wage of Rs. 5 per day on 4-9-78 and he worked as such 
for 101 days, 56 days in 1978 and 45 days in 1979. He 
sealed his account with Gonda Branch of the bank without 
any demur or protest. So he is stopped in raising any dispute 
m his regard. After a lapse of more than 2 years he was 
engaged as a casual worker on daily wage of Rs 7 on U P. 
Civil Seett, Branch Lucknow, for a total period of 88 days 
from I2-I0-8I to 3-2-82 by way of separate distinct and 
fresh casual employment. Here also the workman settled 
Ws account without any demur or protest. As such he is 
stopped in raising any d'spule in the matter, The period of 
indcpcndant working at Gonda and Lucknow cannot be 
clubbed together. 

5. The management further plcad.s that the lelief .sought is 
hot maintainable us termination of services of rbe workman 
does not amount to retrenchment withffi the meaning of 
Industrial Disputes Act. According to tffc management para 
16.9 of the Desai Award lays down that petsons who are 
casually employed or employed to do job work are e.xcludcd 
from the operation of the Award. Even otherwise the refe¬ 
rence is not maintainable inasmuch as the workman was 
considered for regular absorption in the continuous service 
by an Interview Board before which the workman appeared 
on 7-11-85 but was found unsuitable. 



3620 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : AUGUST. 11, JOyO/SRAVANA 20, 1912 [Part H- 3(ii)l 


6. On, 9-3-89, the workman applied, for amendment of 
edaim statement and his amendment application wai allied. 
THo,amendment was with regard to the period of working at 
Gonda Branch of the bank. It was alleged by him that he ftad 
worked at tire said branch from 17-7-78 to i6-7-79. He was 
allowed to mark attendance from 17-7-78 to 4-6-79, but 
thereafter, he was not allowed to mark his attendance. He 
was asked to submit bil,8 for the work done bv him ranging 
rrdm 5 to 7 days. He also allege that no letter of appoint¬ 
ment was given to him. He was again appointed on 16-5-81), 
and worked till 30;.‘'-80. In annexoro I/A he has given the 
permd of his working at Gonda Branch till 16-7-79, in 
amiexurc I/B he has given the details of Sundays and holidays 
which had intervened during the alleged period of bis working 
annexure 1/C gives the details of payment received by the 
workman in respect of days diii'ing wli ch he had worked In 
the months of June and July 1979 and annexiitc 1/D showing 
that he had v.orked for a total number of 265 days, 

7. fu their additional written statement, the management 
reiterated the facts alleged by them in their earlier written 
statement, 

8. According to the managemenl, the workman, while chaJ- 
lenging the different engagement of his casual employment 
from Gonda Branch of the bank, has travelled beyond tbl 
scope and limit of the reference order. Therefore, the 
amended claim statement filed by him is liable to be rejected. 
T'he management also deny that the workman was engaged 
for a continuous period of 6 days so as to earn one day pa'd 
holiday inasmuch as the casual engagement was on day to 
day basis depending on job requirement at the branch. The 
manftgement has Ohee again denied that he was engaged as 
a messenger in the sub staff. 

9. In support of his case, the workmim has filed his own 
affidavit and some documents. There is no evidence from 
The 'side of the /lanagement. 

10. It has been rightly contended by Shri Mahesh Chandra, 
tHe authorised representative for the management, that the 
working of the two brandies Gonda Branch and U.P. Civil 
fieett. Branch cannot be clubbed as the working at the said 
two branches are different and independent of each other. 
It is also not the case of the workman that his engagement/' 
appointment at U.P. Civil Scett. Brunch Lucknow was in 
continuation fi liis working at Gonda Branch, In fact even 
according to his own showing his second period of working 
at Gonda Branch was from 16-5-80' to 30-5-80. Therefore, 
his working at Gonda Branch cannot be taken into account 
whiC detTmining tb" is'-uo ref'rr'-d to tli's T-hun"' '^or 
adjud'eation by the Min stry of ILabour, New Delhi. 'What 
is required of this Tribunal is to see whether or not the 
action of the management in terminating tlic services of the 
workman w.e.f. 3-2-82 from the U.P. Civil Seett. Branch 
Lucknow is justified, 

11. Sbrl R. K, Pandey the authori.scd veDicsenlnlive for 
the workman has made a vain attempt to btiild up an entirely 
new case of the workman on the basis of his. working at 
Gonda Branch, 

12. Since even according to the own showing of the work¬ 
man ho had worked for only 88 days during the j-tcriod of 12 
.months preceding the date of his termination at U.P. Civil 
Seeft. Branch of the State Bank of India, tiie quesibni of 
voilatirtg the provisions of Section 2.5-F I. D. Act, in the 
present case does not arise, 

13. Even at Gonda Branch on the basis of details given 
by him he cannot be said to have worked for 240 days or 
more within a period of 12 months preceding the date of 
his termination. We have seen that at fir.st he came out 
with the case that he had worked there for ]01 days dur¬ 
ing the periods 17-7-78 to 31-5-79. In siipnort of thi.s 
fact he relied upon the certificate dated 19-1-87 irsued 
by the branch manager Gonda Branch, copy oi wliich is 
annexure T to bis original claim stalemenl. 

-14. In the original claim statement he also alleged that 
he was again appointed on 16-5-80, but it was not stated 
by him as to till what time be continued in his subsequent 
appointment. Later on by mc:ini of amendment application 


he clarified the position and stated that he had worked from 
'6-5-80 to 30-5-80. Therefore, the relevant period to be 
LonaiJered is 31-5-79 to 30-5-80. During this period the 
ngurcs given by him do not make out a case for him that he 
nad worked for 240 days or more during tlic said period. 
Therefore, it cannot be said that even at Gonda Branch, 
the management was guilty of noj compliance with the provi¬ 
sions of Section 25-F I. D. Act. 

15. I may state hero that during the holding of camp court 

at Lucknow on May 24. 1990, Shri Pandey, the authciised 
icpresentative for the workman argued the case in the 
absence of the authorised representative for the management. 
.Much reliance was placed. on by him on the fact that 
during the period 17-7-78 to 16-7-79 he had worked for more 
man 240 days at Gonda Branch. In support of his conten¬ 
tion he relied On Annexurcs l/A lol/D. Even though there is 
not rtiuch relevance of it, r,rili on going through these state¬ 
ments I find that die figures arrived at bj- him in Annexiue 
1/D do not seem to be correct. Annexure I/B refers to 
the list of Sundays and holidays failing during the said period. 
It will appear that some Sundays such as 23-7-78, 6-8-78, 
13-8-78, 22-8-78, 22-10-78 and 4-2-79 have already been 

taken into account in the statement of working days given 
in Annexure I/A, Then other days have also been added 
towards number of working days, .although the workman was 
not entitled to those holid.rys Section 9 of LhP, Shops 
and Commercial Establishment Act lays down that every 
employee shall be allowed b\ the err>,ployct' one holiday in 
e i,:h week. The proviso lays down that it will not apply 
to an employee whose total period of employment in the 
week including any day spent on leave or any holiday is 
Ic-ss than 6 days. Thus list of 66 days of holidays in the 
light of what has been said above will get reduced to 26 
days or a little more. In annexure i.o. he has shown 25 days 
of working. But this is not supported by workman's own 
document dated 16-10-89 which is said to be the statement 
prepared on the basis of Bank’s register. It w'ill appear 
from the entries noted at serial No. 17 and 18 that between 
31-5-79 and 16-7-79 the workman had worked only for 
11 days and not for 25 days. The total period of working 
will come of near about 210 days. 

16. Thus in any case the vollation of Section 25-F I. D. 
Act ^s not stand proved. 

17. The workfnan has also alleged voilaiion of Section 25-0 
I. D. Act and 25-H of I. D. Act read with Rules 76, 77 and 
78 I. D. Central Rules 1957. In paras 10' and 12 of his 
affidavit he has refeiTcd the persons who had been appointed 
aft3 termination of his services at Gonda Branch and not 
5h U.P, Civil Seett. Branch. Nothing has been deposed to 
by him in his affidavit that junior hands were retained at 
the time of termination of his services. In the circiiraslances, 
the question of vollation of Section 25-G I. D. Act does not 
arise. 

18. Section 25-H read with Rule 78 refers to cases of those 
woiTmen who had worked continuously for nor less than one 
year before termination of their services. This be'ng not 
-SO in the case of the present workman, the quostion of voila- 
tion of Section 25-H read with Rule 78 also does.pot arise. 

19. Hence, in view of the above discus Ions of facts and 
circumstances, I hold that the action of the State Bank of 
India Lucknow in relation to their U.P. Civil Seett. Branch 
Lucknow in terminating the .services of the workman w'.e.l. 
3-2-82 is fully jirstified. Consequently, the workman is 
entitled to no relief. 

20. Reference is answered accordingly.

diistnal dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication tinder sec¬

“Whether the action of the management of International 
Airport Authority of India, Bombay in terminatins 
the services of' Mr. S. A.' Rthmali, Driver U.e.f. 
21-7-87 is legal and justified;^ If rmt. what relief is 
the workman entitled to ?” 

2 This reference has been pending for over a year now. 
The workman,'has not cared tr> file his statement of claim, 
although as many as 10 adjournments have teen made to- 
date. Today he has remained absent. It appear.s thi t he 's 
not in vested iri pursuing his claim. In absence of any mate¬ 
rials. I liold that the workman has failed to prove that the 
action taken against him is illegal or unjustiflod. ResuUantly 
he is not entitled to any relief. Award accordingly. No 
order as to costs.

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 26th July, 1990 

S.O. 2157: — Where.is an industrial dispute exists between the management of Associated 
Cement Companies Limited, Chanda Cement Works, P.O. Cementnagar, Distt. Chandrapur (M.S.) 
and their workmen represented by Vidarbha Stone Mine Workers Union, P.O. Sindola Mine Tq. Wani, 
Distt. Yeotnual (M.S.); 

And whereas, the said employers and their workmen have by a written agreement under Section 
10 A of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), agreed to refer the said dispute to Arbitration and 




[WFr 11--^ ,1 (ii)] ■ . tpT Tinna : 11 W’Tm, 199o/«fm 20, 1912 3629 

have forwarded to the Central Government under sub-section(3) of section 10-A of the said Act, a copy of 
the said arbitration agreement; 

Now, There7ore, in pursuance of sub-section (3) of Section 10-A of the said Act, the Central 
Government hereby publishes the said agreement: 

AGREEMENT 

(Under Section 10-A of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947) 

Between 

Name of the parties: 

Representing Employers The Associated Cement Co. S. Ltd., Chanda Cement 

Works. 

Representing Workmen: Vidarbha Stone Mine Workers Union. 

It is hereby agreed between the parties to refer the following disputes to the arbitration of Shri M.N. 
Chandurkar, Ex-Chief Justice, High Court at Bombay & Madras, 10-B ‘SAMATA’, Jen. J. Bhonsale Marg, 
Bombay-400 021. 

(i) Specific matters in disputes : Whether the Demand of General Secretary, Vidarbha 

Stone Mines Workers’ Union, Sindola Mines, Yeot- 
mal District, asking the management of A.C.C. Ltd. 
for holding enquiry and grant suitable punishment 
to Shri Surat Singh Havildar for alleged misbeha¬ 
viour with the wife of Shri Prithvichand Chopra, 
Watchman on 27-1-90 is proper, valid and legal? 

If so, then what action is to be taken in the matter? 

(ii) .Details of the parties to the dispute including The Associated Cement Cos. Ltd., Chanda 
the name & address of the establishment or Cement Works P.O. Cementnagar-442 502. Dist. 
undertaking involved Chandrapur (M.S.) 

(iii) Name of the workman in case he himself is Vidarbha Stone Mine Workers Union, P.O. Sindola 

involved in the dispute or the name of the Mines, Tq. Wani, Dist. Yeotmal (M.S.) 

Union, if any, representing the workman in 
question 

(iv) Total number of workmen employed in the 223 
undertaking alfected 

(v) Estimated number of workmen affected or 223. 
likely to be affected by the dispute 

The arbitrator shall make his award within a period of 3 months from the date of notification in the 
Official Gazette or within such further time as is extended by mutual agreement between us in writing. In case 
the award is not made within the period aforementioned, the reference to arbitration shall stand automatically 
cancelled and we shall be free, to negotiate for fresh arbitration. 

Representing employer; Representing workmen ;

cerned workman is entitled to?

shall Kishore has been raised by Uttar Railway Kararachari

Tho workman had thus worked for more than 240 days in

compliance of Seciion 25F l.D. Act, iy47. 

3. The management wliile admitting the fact tliat the 
workman was appointed as a casual labour on 10-11-75 plead 
the woikman had worked upto 14-6-77 in the said capacity 
in broken periods against sanctions and dificrent nature oi 
jobs. The management deny the fact that during the year 
preceding 15-6-77, tlie workman had worked for 240 days 
or more . This fact is denied also on the ground of non¬ 
availability of paid vouchers which under Rules could be 
preserved only for a period of 5 years. The long absence of 
7 years without putting up any claim for job or compensa¬ 
tion goes to show that the workman did not turn up tor 
further employment, after 14-6-77. He must jjave some 
other employment in his mmd. In any case, the claim is 
barred by time. The management further deny that the ser¬ 
vices of the workmen were ever terminated. Hence, the 
question of compliance of Sec. 25F l.D. does not arise. 

4. In the rejoinder it is alleged by the Union iniit no 
period of limitation is prescribed under the l.D. Act fot 
raising an industrial dispute. Further paid vouchers arc also

snanagement. Lastly, the Union alleges that the management

6 . On 17-U-86, my learned predecessor Shri R. B, Sri- 
vasiava, gave award in favour of the Union. He ordered

after lioldmg that the workman liad worked for more than 
240 days during the period of one yeqr prior to termination 
of his services; that he had acquired termporary status by

time as no period of limitation is prescribed under the f.D. 
Act and tliat the workman was entitled to back wages. 

7. Against the said award the Railway Administration filed

The Hon’ble High Court permitted the parties lo adduce

that the photostat copy of the casual labour card filed by 
the Union/workman fully satisfy almost all the requirements 
of the particulars and specimen given in para 2513 of the 
Manual. 


18. I may slate that even during the comsc of arguments, 
it was never submitted by Shri S. P. S. Chauhan the autho¬ 
rised representative for the management that the casual labour 
card which is filed known as service card of a casual worker 
is not in the form in which the photostat copy mentioned 
above is. No attempt has been made from the side of the 
management to show that the casual labour card is in some 
other form. The Tribunal can take judicial notice of the 
fact that in many cases against the railway to which railway 
is a party, this type of service cards have been treated by 
both sides as casual labour cards. 


19. Column No, 1 refers to SI. Number and the main 
heading of column Nos. 2 and 3 refers to period of employ¬ 
ment (from to). So column Nos. 2 and 3 only icfct.s to tire 
period during which a casual labour had actually worked 
and not the period of work in a particular sanction. 

20. The entries appearing in the photostat copy of the 
casual labour card filed with his affidavit by the workman 
fully tally with the periods given by the Union in para 2 
of the claim statement as wcU as the periods of working 
given in the reply of the Railway AdminisTration filed by 
Shri V. P. Arya, lOW before RLC (C) Kanpur. The Tri¬ 
bunal is not to enter into the controversey as to how Shri 
Arya came across these periods. If the Railway Adfin-stralion 
was of the view that there was no basis for Shri Arya for 
giving these periods the Railway Administiation should have 
put Shri Arya in the witness box and after declaring him 
hostile inquired about from him. The very fact that entries 
regarding the period of actual work are made in the casual 
labour card by the officers of the Railway Administration 
entries cannot be di.sbelieved unless it is shown that these 
entries are fake or forged or have been made by some un¬ 
authorised person. 


21. It thus becomes evident from the above documentary 
evidence coupled with the evidence of the workman that the 
workman had worked for 267 days during the period of 
one year preceding the date Of terminafTon of his services 
i.e. 15-6-77. No reliance can be placed on the testfinony 
of Shri V. N. Kapoor who in his cross examination has 
said that in the casual labour card filed by the Union periods 
of work sanctioned have only been noted. Muster Roll 
entries and paid vouchers arc no doubt important, but they 
are simply corroborative piece of evidccc. Casual Labour 
card is a document which is delivered to the workman by 
the Railway Administration and in which entries are made 
by the officers of the Railway to show as to during what 
period (he workman had actually worked. The sanctity of 
the entries, therefore, remains there. 


22. Hence from the evidence on record T hold that the 
Union has been able to prove that during the period of one 
■year preceding the date of termination, the workman had 
worked for 267 days. 


23. Here T would like to refer to the plea taken up by 
the managemenct in para 5 of the written statement. In 
fhc said para it is pleaded that the long absence of 7 years 
without any claim for job or compensation goes to prove 
that the workman did not turn up for further employment 
under lOW Alambagh Lucknow. Tn other words accordin,'! 
to The management, the workman left the ioh of his own 
accord. There appears to he no substance or force in this 
plea. Had it been so. the workman would not have raised 
the dispute 'n 1977 and 1978 as has been admitted by the 
management witness Shri Dayal in his cross examination 
Point No. 2 is decided accordingly. 






3635 


[«TTfl II —3 (ii)] ii, 20, 1912 


Point No. 3 

24. No such plea has been taken by the RIy. Administration 
in the written statement. Even otherwise the Railway is an 
expanding department and there Is no death of jobs of 
casual nature- for a person like the present workman. So I 
find no force in the contention that no post of casual labour 
exist for taking back Shri Kaushal K.iahore the present work¬ 
man. Point No. 3 is decided accordingly. 

Point No. 4 

25. As remarked by me earlier on point No. 1 , wc have 
to consider the case of back wages with regard to the 
period 1979 to May 1984. Even after rejection of his first 
representation he should have persued his remedy in a court 
of law either himself or through the Union. The fact that 
he approached the Union very late or that he came to know 
fhat he had a genuine cause of action very late is no excuse. 
Had he taken up the matter in all earnestness soon after 
rejection of his representation in the event of success, the 
railway could have utilised his services during the above 
period. Therefore, I hold that the workman is not entitled 
to back wages for the period 1979 to May 1984, 

26. At page 20 of the copy of judgment, the Hon'blo 
High Court has observed as follows— 

These authorised do lend support to the contention of 
the learned counsel for the petitioner that where 
the worker has earned emoluments else where, the 
said emoluments are liahio to he deducted. 

The above observation was made by the Hon’ble High 
Court when it was submitted before it from the side of 
the Railway Administration that in his cro.ss examinaion 
the workman had stated that he had been maintaining him¬ 
self by earning Rs. 12 or 13 per day as agrtinst the wage of 
Rs. 6.50 paise per day paid hy the Railways to him, Tn 
his cro.ss examination before reman'd of the case, the work¬ 
man has deposed that he got Rs. 12 or Rs 13 a day when 
he got daily work. In his cross examination after remand. 
The workman has deposed that after termination of his services 
some times he earned a early wage of Rs, 10 or Rs. 12. 

27. There is no evidence from the side of the management 

that as a casual labour, the workman was getting Rs- 6.50 
paise per day. Even if it is admitted that his daily wage 
was Rs, 6.50 pa]se a day it is not clear whether it was 
inclusive of all admissible allowance or exclusive of such 
allowances. The statement of workman on which reliance 
has been placed from the management side do not go to 
prove fhat all along, the workman remained engaged and 
had been earning Rs. 12 or Rs. 13 a day. To my mind, 
this question can be belter examined in proceedings u/s 

33C(2) I. D. Act. when on the basis of award given in 

Ihe prc.sent case, the workman files an application for 

Tecovery of money benefits. For the moment payment of' 
Back wages can be made conditional on his furnishing an 
affidavit that bi- bad not remained in gainful employment 
after the termination of his services. J therefore, leave the 
point with regard to back wages here with the above 
observations. 

28. ft has been admitted by Shri Kapoor towards the end 

of his cross examination that wh'lc termination his services 
no notice was given nor any retrenchment compensation was 
paid of the workrnnn Having worked for more than 240 davs 
during the period of one year preceding the dale of his 

terminat'on, the termination vViuld he deemed as void ab'nitio 
on account of non compliance of the .-rovicions of s'<.cfion 
25-F I n Act. The Union ha^ also placed re’iance on .Section 
'’S-G in A-t hut (he Tribunal need not take notice of if 
hecaus- no such plea was taken hy the Union either in the. 
claim siafement or in the reioinder For the first time the 
plea ha: been raised 'n the affidavil of (he workman. In his 

Ptoft heto'-? remand of 'he hr has :aid that 

’unior hands wefr retained wh'le terminating his services. 
Bnt name of no iunior was given bv him in his affidavit, 1 
therefore find no force in this plea of the Union. 

29 In view of th-' flndmgs given above, it is held that 
ttii- action of the management m tenninating the services of 
Shri Kaushal Kishore w.c.f, 15-6-77 is neither legal nor 


justified. Consequently, the workman is ended to reinstate¬ 
ment and back wages except for the period 1979 to May, 
1984, on his furnishing an affidavit to the effect that he 
was not gainfully employed any where else after the termt- 
naiion of his services, before the management. 

30. Reference is answered accordingly.

appointment on 15-11-79, However, when again questioned

of his statement that he got appointment in November 1979. 
He did not Rive the date. From where date 15-11'79 was 
taken is not known specially when no such date was men¬ 
tioned in tlie claim statement or rejoinder. 

13. In para 6 of his statement in cross examination he 
ha.s deposed that after termination of his services he never 
made any representation to any Railway Officer that his 
services' had been lermijMted illegally. He has admitted llie 
fact in para .5 of his statement in cross examination that on 
4-10-81 with him the services of many other persons were 
terminated. This is y haf has been stated by the manage¬ 
ment in parn 4 of tlie written statement. The management 
have contended that all the casual labours whose services 
were terminated w.c.f. 4-10-81 were not engaged by a com¬ 
petent aiitliority. Then in para 6 of his statement in cross 
examination he has deposed that the dispute on his behalf 
was raised before Assistant Labour Commissioner (Centrall 
in 198.5. The question is why he did not make any repre¬ 
sentation to higher officer of the Railway against nis illegal 
termination and why he waited ti] 1985. There is no expla¬ 
nation for it. 

14. From the side of the Union a number of documents 
have been filed in support of the case of Shri Singh. Anne- 
xure I, to the affidavit ot Shri Singh is photostat copy of a 
portion of his identity card, At the time of his cross exami¬ 
nation original Identity Card was nlso got filed from him. 
According to him, the Identitv Card fcopyl bears the signa¬ 
tures of Shri V. N. Dwivedi whom he has been making sJgtio- 
fures. He also 'late that on the inside of ibe original Identity 
Card there also anpear the signatures of Shri Dwivodf, There 
is a observation by me soon after this statement of Sfiii 
Singh that the two signatures vastly differ. He has said 
that Shri Dwivedi was n clerk who use to do booking. I 
don’t think that an Identity Card would have been issued 
to him with the signatures of a clerk. An Identity Card 
issued in the name of an employee normally bears the signa¬ 
tures of the officer concerned. Therefore, to me the Identity 
that in para fi of in’s affidavit the management witness has 
deposed that Shri Sin.eh was never issued any Identity Curd 
hy a competent authority. In view of the facts stated above 
tlie re appears to be force in it. 

Card appears to ho, of doubtful character, T may shite here 

15. Reliance has then bo placed from the side of the Union, 
on photostat copies of pay slips which arc annexures V to 
Annexure.s XIV of the affidavit of Shri Singh. It has been 
admitted hy Shri Singh in para 7 of his statement in cross 
c.vamination that except anncxnre X no other pay slip bears 
the signatures of any railway employee or officer. As regards 
annexiu e Xll, ho says that he cannot say whose initials appear 
on it. AVith regard to it, the management witne.ss has deposed 
in para 6 that tliese pay slips have never been issued by 
any competent aulhority. They appear to be fake. In his 
cross examination, the management witness has denied that 
nay slips arc issued witlioiit the signatures of the competent 
authority. In the circumstances, the genuineness of lliese 
pay slips riso appear to be very much in doubt. 

16. Reliance lias then been placed on the photostat copy 
of chaigcshcci .r.iiic.xurc IV-A to the affidavit of Shri Singh. 
Shri P. N. Shiikla, who was examined after the close of 
management evidence by the Union, admitted that annexure 
IV 7 A bears his signature. He has clearly stated that he 
does not know Shri Singh personally. When during the course 
of cross examination, Shri Shukla was shown Shri Singh, 
Shri Shukla slated that it was only that very day outside the 
court room that he was told that he was Shri Singh. Prior 
to that he had not been knowing Shri Singh, In the charge- 
sheet, the father’s name of Shri Shamblyu Dayal, Callman 
Loco Shed Is not given. Anoth^ Important fact to be noted 
in this connection is that whereas the Union has given the 
name of Shri Singh ns Shri Shnmbhu Daynl Singh, In the 


chai'gcblicet the vvorkniaii charged has been named as Shambhu 
Dayal not at one place but at 6 places. Had he been tlie 
same person lie would have been described by bin full name 
i.e. Shambhu Dayal Singh. 

17. Lastly, reliance has been placed on photostat copies 
of Passes and PIU, annexure II and 111 to the affidavit of 
Shri Singli. In his affidavit llic manageinont witness has de¬ 
posed that these two documents are fake, lie ha.s also de¬ 
posed that passc.s are not issucii for such long period js 
lias been shown in the photostat copy of the pa.sscs held by 
Sbri Single The date of issue is given as tl-6-3fJi utr.l the 
oalc of availability i.s gi\en as 10-10-80, 

18. In view of so many circumshmees appt\i:tng against 
•Sbri Siiieb much reliance cannot be placed on these two docu¬ 
ments. If he could procure other document^, it wa.s not 
difficnlt fin- him to have procu/ed these two documents, ;dso. 

19. From the above evidence and circumstances, I thus 
find that ti-,c Union has not been able to establish by cogent 
and reliable evidence that Shri Singh was ever recruited/ 
engaged as Callman by AME ((Loco), Lucknow. In the cir¬ 
cumstances, the question of termination of his services and 
tile que.stion of compliance with the provisions of Section 
2.5F I. D Act, do not arise. 

20. Hence, Shri Singh is held entitled to no relief. 

21. Reference is answered accordingly.

AWARD 

1 The Central Government, Ministry of lithour, vide its 
notificat.’on No, 42012/118/8S-D-3(B), dated 24 5 1989. has 



['Ti r 11—-^ 3 (ii)]

<rrw Sn WWi 11, 1990'«IT1^ 20, 1913 


refericd ttic fo)]owinfi dispute for adjudication to this 
Tribunal ;— 

Whetiitir lilt li.\ccutivo Hngiueer CPWD Hot Mix Plant 
ILucktiow Division wag, justified in retrenching Shri 
Jogeshv ar Prasad w.c.f. 12-10-87 V If not, to what 
relief ihe workman was entitled ? 

2. The industrial dispute on behalf of the workman has 
been raised by CPWD Mazdoor Union (hereinafter referred 
to as Unionj. The case of the Union is that the workman 
had been working on Kachcha Chliitha since 9-12-85, but 
w.e.f. 17-10-86, without any reason or ryhme, his name was 
removed from Kachcha Chhitha and he was made to work 
on payment of wages against Dasti receipts. His services 
were terminated w.e.f. 12-10-87. On 18-10-87, he was given 
a notice by the management in which it was stated that his 
services had been terminated since the road work had been 
completed. The Union alleges that whereas other workers 
like the workman had been absorbed in other Mandals no¬ 
thing has been done by the management in respect of the 
workman. The Union, has therefore, jirayed for his absorp¬ 
tion. 

3. The case is contested by Executive Engincor(E), 
Lucknow Cantt. Eiecirical Division, CPWD, Lucknow. The 
management plead that the name of Shri Jogeshwar Prasad 
was sponsored hy the Employment Exchange on 7-1-86 and 
he was selected besides other candidates for Hot Mix Plant 
Division tHMPD). Diiiing the execution of road works, the 
workman worked for 22 days from 9-12-85, to 4-1-86 and 
for 190 days from 3-12-86 to 12-10-87 on Hand receipt 
and for 229 days from 10-1-86 to 17-10-86 on Mustor Roll. 
.\cording to the management Shri Jogeswar Pd. was vicll 
aware of the fact that his employment on hand receipt was 
of casual and temporary nature. On completion of road work 
his sen’icc.s were tornnnated on 12-10-87 as no road woik 
was left with the Division. As per provisions of Secnon 25F 
1. D. Act Shri Joge-hwar Prasad was informed to collect the 
payment of compensation due to him, but he refused to 
collect it. According to his no person junior to Shri logsshwar 
Prasad was taken in employment as per Seniority List issued 
by Ex. Engineer (E), Kanpur Central Electrical Division, 
Copy of the Seniority List has been filed with the written 
statement. 

4. In the lejoinder the receipt of notice with regard to 
collection of retrenchment compensation is admitted by the 
Union. The Union alleges that from the seniority list it 
will bo evident that whereas Shri Bal Krishna Ray was ap¬ 
pointed on 20-12-85, Shri .Jogeshwar Prasad was appointed on 
9-12-85. Thus the latter was senior to the former. It means 
th.nt thi Principle of Last Come First Go was not followed. 

5. In support of its case, the Union has filed the affidavit 
of the workman and some documents. On the other hand, 
the management, in support of their case, have filed the affi¬ 
davit of Shri Pramod Kumar Garg, Executive Engineer (E), 
Lucknow, and some documents. 

6. The first and foremost question to bo examined in (he 
case is whether or not tliere was any vollation of Section 25F 
1. D. Act. The Union’s case is that the woikinan had worked 
on Muster Roll from 9-12-85 to 16-10-86 and on hand receipt 
from 17-10-86 to 12-10-87. For the purposes of finding out 
whether or not there was voilation of section 25F I. D, Act 
it is Immaterial whether the workman had worked on muster 
roll or on hand receipt. The two periods will have to be 
clubbed let us examine the Union’s case in this light. 

7. Point for determination, therefore, is whether Shri 
Jogeshwar Prasad had w'orked for 240 days or more during 
the period 13-10-86 to 12-10-87. With his affidavit the work¬ 
man has filed the photostat copy of the certificate showing the 
period and the number of days during which he had worked. 
The document has been admitted by the management and it 
has been marked Ext. W-l. The photostat copy of this docu¬ 
ment has also been filed by the Union with the claim state¬ 
ment, The certificate wh-cli is dated 28-12-87 has been issued 
by Executive Engineer (FI HMPD, Lucknow, In hi.s cross 
examination,' the v.'orkman has deposed that he had worked 
for those number of days as has been .shown in the ceitifl- 
cale. 


8. From the certificate it appears that during the period 
3-12-86 to 12-10-87 lie hud worked for 190 days and during 
the period 13-10-86 to 17-10-86 ho had worked for 5 days 
i.c. dining the period 13-10-86 to 12-10-87 he had worked 
only for 195 day.s. Having worked for much le.,s than 240 
days during the period of 12 months preceding tile date of 
termination of his services, the question of compliance of 
section 23F l.D. Act by tlic niaiiageinent does not arise, Ihc 
notice which is dated 18-1U-87 which was admittedly given 
hy the management to the workman for the purpose of 
collecting retrenchment compensation is of no consequence. 
The photostat copy of the notice dt. 18-10-87 has also been 
filed by the wiukman wiih his affidavit and it is Ext. W-2. 

9. The .second point to be considered is whether or Lot 
the management voilatcd the provisions of section 25G I. D. 
Act. in me claim statement it is no where sL.,i.ed oy l.io 
U nion that while terminating the services of the workman 
junior hands were retained in service by the management. 
All tliat has been alleged in para 6 of the claim statement is 
that the workman like Shri Jogeshwar Prasad had been 
absorbed in otlicr Divisions. So on the basis of the facts 
alleged in the claim statement no case of voilation of section 
25G 1. D. Act is made out. It was only for the fii'st lime 
that in para 4 of the rejoinder it had been alleged by the 
LInion that Sliri Bal Krishna Ray was junior to Shri Jogeshwar 
Prasad, their dates of appointment are given as 20-12-85 
and 9-12-85. In it also 1 find no force. Reliance has been 
placed fiom the side of the Union on the list of Muster 
Roll. From the said list it appears that Shri Ray, 'oclongs to 
the category of A.s.sistant wireman and Shri Jogeshwar Prasad 
belonged to the ciilcgory of Khalasi. The two categories 
being different, the queslion of application of section 25G 
I, D. Act docs not arise, Similarly the dates which arc 
given in column No. 6 are the dates on which these two 
persons started working on muster roll and not the dates 
on which they were originally employed. From the two dates 
it will appear that Shri Jogeshwar Piasad was brought on 
mu.ster roll much after Shri Ray, In his affidavit no such 
fact with regard to SJiri Ray has been deposed lo by the 
workman. 

10. In his cross exanfination, the workman came out with 
9 different stand. In para 3 of the statement in his cross 
c.xaraination, the workman has deposed that Shri Kamlesh 
Kumar who was junior to him was allowed to continue In 
service while his services were terminated. He has expressed 
his ignorence on the point whether or not Shri Kamlesh Kumar 
had worked on hand receipt from Juno or July 1985, He 
named one more pei'son by the name of Shri Umesn Kumar 
Dixit who according to him has been adjusted in Civil 
Division. 

11. Thus there is nothing to show that there was voilation 
of section 250 ]. D. Act by the management, rather as 
earlier stated troni the claim statement It appears that with 
the completion of road work as was stated in the notice 
Ext. W-2 other woikers were absorbed in other Divisions. 
So even in case the Union has been able to prove voilation 
of section 25F, the question of retrenchment wuold not have 
iiriien. 

12. Hence, from the above discussion of evidence and 
find'ngs, it is held that the action of the Executive Engineer, 
CPWD, HMPD, Lucknow in retrenchment Shri Jogeshwar 
Prasad w.e f. 12-10-87 cannot bo held as unjustified. The 
Union/workman is consequently held to no relief. 

13. Reference is answered acqprdingly.

'fhe management has quoted in cxtenso in his W.S. 
the relevant decision of the JBCCI regarding the review/ 
determination of date t)f birth of existing employees and 
Ihe said provision cannot be denied. Jt will appear from the 
said provision that whenever there is no variation in the 
age/date of birth in the records such cases will not be re- 
opened unless there is glaring apparent wrong entry brought 
to the notice of the management. It is only when the manage¬ 
ment being satisfied on the merits of the case that it wilt 
take appropriate action for correcting through age determi¬ 
nation committee/medical board, II is further provided 
that whenever there are variation suitable provision for age 
determination committee/medical board will be made. In 
the present case there docs not appear to be any variation In 
the records of the management or any document produced 
by tlje management regarding the date of birth of the con¬ 
cerned wbikman and' as such according to JBCCi the case 
brought out by the workmen does not appear To be fit for 
ciimeting his age by the age detcrmination/medical board. 
There is no evidence to show fhat there is any glaring and 
apparent wrong entry in respect of the date of birth of the 
concerned workman in the management's record. 

fn view of the discussions made above it is apparent that 
it is not a fit cast in which the case of the concerned work¬ 
man should be referred To the age deierm'nation committee/ 
medical board for the determination of the age. The manage¬ 
ment has superannuated him on the basis of the date of 
birth recorded in the management's records which have no 
variation in it and there is no glaring or apparent wrong entry 
in it. 

In the result, I hold that the action of the management 
of Sudatndlh Coal Washery of Sudamdih Area of IVf/s, 
BCCT in superanTmritlng the concerned workman Shri Govind 
Satnitmi. General ^fazdoor wi^ effect from l6-7-8fi is histl- 
fled and consequently the concerned workman is entitled to 
no relief, 

This Is my Award. 

I. N. SINHA, Presiding Officer

2. The case of the employer in relation to the management 
of Bhurungia Project of M/s. Bharat Coking Coni Ltd., as 
di.sclosed in the written itatement, details apart, is as foLov-s: 

Bhurungia Project of M/s. B.C.C.Ltd., comprises of four 
nnit .9 of closed coal mines, namely, Bhurungia colliery, 
Ranidih/Pipratand colliery, Pathergoria colliery and 
East Muchraidih Colliery. The ex-owner of Bhurungia colliery 
2042 GI/90—13 


totally closed the mine on 21-2-1962 and removed, all ma¬ 
chines, i]4rucLures, head-gear etc. from the,,colliery. The pits 
were water-lodged and (he ex-owner surrendored/ftuctioiiod the 
ktuc lung ago. Bhuiungia Colliery wat neither taken over 
nor was it nationalised under the provisions of the Coking 
Co.il Mines il-mcrgeucy Provisions) Aot, 1971, the Coking 
Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1972 or the Coal Mines 
(Nalunialisaiionj Act, 1973. M/s, Ltd. applied to the 

Stale Govermnent of Bihat in accordance with the Mineral 
Goticc.ssioii Rules, 1960 for a giant of mming lease of the 
coal bc.ir.ng area ,, for i.iiMiig coal and on 29-7-75 an agree¬ 
ment w.as arrived at by and between the State of Bihar and 
M/s. B.C.C.Ltd., In terms of which the Government of 
Bihar leased 738 biglms of coal bearing land in favour of 
M/k. B.C.C. Ltd., The coal below ground of Bhurungia Pro¬ 
ject is of the same type i.e. Mohuda Top Seam and is 
medium coking coal and was by and large virgin M/s. 
B.C.C. Ltd. wanted to use and supply this typo of Coal to 
the Steel Projects and other industrial units in the country. 
VVitli this end in view a Project called BJiurungia 
Project was stated comprising of Ranidih/Plprutand, 
Bhurungia, Pathergoria and East Muchraidih, collieries. The 
first phase of the project was re-opening of Ranidih/Pipratand 
unit which was partly started on 1-1-75. Production frdht 
titat unit started in Pcbi'uaty, 1976, Tlie work of the second 
phase i.e. dewatering of Bhurungia Unit Pit No 3 was started 
in May, 1976 i.e. fitting of head gear, installatitm of winder 
and sub-station at Pit No, 6 was completed in June, 1977. 
Production from Ranidih/Pipratand was started on 15-5-1980 
as its working was connected with the workings of Bhurungia 
No. 5 Pit, Under the piovisions of Coal Mines Regulations. 
1937, Ranidih/Pipratand Unit and Bhurungia Unit became 
one mine and thereafter pioduction at Pit No, 5 of Blmrungla 
Project started. Pit No. 6 of Bhurungia is still under the 
project stage and the dia of the pit is widened from the 
existing abandoned 15 feet to 20 feet. Bhurungia Project 
has not yet started in full. Bhurungia colliery which might 
have existed before did not exist since the date of closure, 
Ministry of Steel & Mines, Department of Coal approved 
the project report prepared by M/s. B.C.C. Ltd, In the

Act ia o special enactment applicable only to the subject

attendance for 250 days or more. According to 
MW-2 R. K. Prasad, tiic job of stone cutting as per 
Wage Board Rccurameiidations includes cutting ol 
drains manually and it is not the job of stone cutter 
to cut sumps, rhis statement of Shri Prasad is again 
inaccurate becauao as per Central Coal Wage Board 
Recommondalinn the jiib description of stone cutter 
is as follows : 

“A w'orkm:,in generally employed in cutting 
drains in the floot strats. making water 
sumps, drivin? stone clrlfis, making shot 
holes m sluiie in preparation for blasting, 
etc." 

This job description of stone cutter is as follows : 

Joint Bip;rjtit 2 CommiUee for the coal industry. 
'I’hus it is evidenced that making wall sumps is a

dence of WW-l Tswarmoni Cope and WW-2 Rahim 
Saikh lirmly establishes the position that they had 
been doing essentially the job of stone cutters. It 
remains to be seen as to how far their evidence is 
buttressed by documentary evidence. 

14. The managemeni has produced a series o[ 
work orders issued to the contractors marked Exts. 
M-2 to M-2iJ9. biU: 17.xts. M-3 scries and forward¬ 
ing letters Exts. M-4 scries. Admittedly, the man¬ 
agement lias not produced the entire work orders 
issued relaiing to th. contractual work assigned lo 
the contractors of the Project, bills and forwarding

contractors were engaged for various types of work, 
such as, letaining iviill, line packing, retaining water 
reserver, drain cutting, brick massionary, debrie,s 
cleaning in sumps, tugger foundation and also mak¬

M-2I6, M-2|8, M-2|9. M-2I10, M-2111, M-2|12, 
M-3 series M-4 series and W-l|l). Thus, the fact 
cannot be ignored that the workmen of the 
contractors which include the concerned workmen 
were em|4oyed for doing stone cutting job. 

WW-l O. P Agarwal has stated that Ext. M-3 
series dated 19-12-83 show that stone cutting job 
was entrusted to the contractors, workmen. But he 
has tried to retrieve the position by stating that all 
works rfdaling to stone cutting job comprise 99% 
uoii-sloiK.' cutling and only 1 % stone cutting job. 
He has had to admit that this has neither been 
written either in tlic bills or in the exhibits in ques¬

iion-siunc cutting job aiiu iy& of stone cutting joo

tne stone cutting. Nevertheless this witness nas ad¬ 
mitted that the Kooi of a nunc is either of stone or 
coal or of shale and floor cutting means cutting of 
tlo-ur which comprises cither of coal or of shale or 
Ol stone. This being the position, the percentage of 
job composition as claimed by him does not stand 
the test of scrutiny. 

15. Thus, the evidence of the witnesses for the 
concerned workmen M-Wl Iswarmoni Gripe and 
MW-d Rahim SaUth that they were employed tor 
doing stone cutting job is buttressed by hard docu¬ 
mentary evidence on record. 

lb. Accordingly to WW-l Iswarmani Gope, stone 
cultmg job is performed in underground mine and 
never on the surface. ITiis statement of fact made 
by him has not been assailed in the cross-examina¬ 
tion. Hence, it is concluded that stone cutting job is 
performed, in underground mine. 

17. The case ol the sponsoring union is that all 
implements for execution of job of stone cutting 
such as, cap lamp, shoes, belts, explosives, hammers, 
stone bits were supplied by the management of the 
Project to the concerned workmen and that the con¬ 
cerned workmen used to work under direct control 
and supervision of the competent authority. Both 
WW-l iswarmani Gope and WW-2 Saikh have 
stated in their testimony that their job as stone 
cutter was of permanent in nature and that the 
management used to provide them work implements 
and the management’s representative used to super¬ 
vise their work. MW-2 R. K. Prasad has admitted 
even ill h’s exarnination-in-chief that the manage¬ 
ment used to supply working tools to tlie contractors’ 
workman and tliat the management used to maintain 
general supervision over work of the contractor, but 
no special supervision used to be made by the 
management. 

MW-1 O. P. Agarwal has admitted that stone 
cutting job is 1 prohibited category of job and the 
contractors’ men cannot be employed for that job. 
Thus the conclusion is reached that although stone 
cutting job by employment of contractors’ workmen is 
a prohibited category of job, the management has 
done it through contractors’ workmen in spite of ban 
or prohibition. 

18. Anyway, from the evidence on record, I 
come to the conclusion that the concerned wnr Vm e.n 
were working as stone cutters which is a perman¬ 
ent nature of job and that the management used 
to provide them with work implements and super¬ 
vise their work. It is also revealed from the evi¬ 
dence that although stone cutting job through con¬ 
tractors’ workmen is prohibited under the provi¬ 
sions of Contract Labour (Regulation & Abolition) 
Act, 1970, the management has resorted to it in 
spite Of this ban or prohibition. 

19. Evidently, the concerned workmen worked 
to produce goods or services for the business of 
Bhurungia Project, 

_Iii the C'Jiitc.'ri of fact;; and circumstances, Shri 
D. MulJierjec, authorised representative for the 
union has contended that since the concerned 




3650 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : AUGUST U. 


workmen were engaged by the Bhurnngia Project 
inrougn contractors in prohibited category of job 
accraicu under tue Contract Labour (ReguJation & 
AnoiiuonJ Act and since the job of Ihc con¬ 
cerned workmen as stone cutter is of permanent 
nature and since their job was used to be super¬ 
vised by the management and the management used 
to provide them with work implements, these 
workmen should be considered to be the workmen 
of the principal employer. As principal employer, 
the management has got economic control over 
their work. In support of his contention Shri D. 
Mukherjee has cited the decision reported in 
A. I. R. 1978 SC. 1410 (Hussainbhai VS. Alath 
Factory Tezhilali Union & others). |rhe ratio of 
the decision cited is that “Where a worker or 
group of workers labours to produce goods or 
services and these goods or services are for the 
business of another, that other is, in fact the em¬ 
ployer. He has economic control over the workers 
subsistance skill and continued employment. If he 
for any reason chocks off, the worker is virtually 
laid off. The presence of intermediate contractor 
with whom alone the workers have immediate or 
direct relationship ex contractu is of no cons¬ 
equence when, on lifting the veil or looking at the 
conspectus of factors governing employment, it is 
found, though droped in different perfect paper, 
ariangement, that the real employer is the man¬ 
agement not the immediate contractor.” 

#ine sponsoring union has submitted a list show¬ 
ing that some of the concerned workmen have 
been continuing to work for the Project while the 
earnings of the other concerned workmen have al¬ 
ready been choked off by the management by stop¬ 
ping them from duty (Ex. W-4). 

Considering the evidence on record I have no 
hesitation to come to the conclusion that the con¬ 
cerned workmen are really the workmen of the 
Project and not of the contractors concerned. 

20. This conclusion is reached from another 
angle also. The combined effect of the provisions 
of Sections 2(2Xb), 7, 12, 20, 21 and 29 of the Con¬ 
tract Labour (Regulation & Abolition) Act, 1970 
make it clear that for a valid employment of cont¬ 
ract labour two conditions must be fulfiled, viz., 
(i) every principal employer of on establishment 
must be registered and (ii) contractojs must have 
a valid licence. In otherwords, the mere registra¬ 
tion by the principal employer or the holding lic¬ 
ence by the contractor alone will not enable the 
management to treat the workmen as contract lab¬ 
our, Breach of these provisions will mean that the 
workmen remained the workmen of the principal 
employer (Current Labour Reports-Vol-3 March 
1990) : United Labour Union & Others VS, Union 
of India and others Bombay High Court.) The 
Hon’blc Madras High Court has also held in a 
deci ion reported in 1985(1) LLJ. 492 (The 
Workmen of Best & Crompton Industries Ltd. VS. 
(i) The management of Best Crompton Eng'nccr- 
ing Ltd., Madras, (ii) The Presiding Officer, 2nd 
Addit'onal Labour Court, Madras and (iii) The 
Workmen of Best & Crompton Engineering Ltd., 
Madras) that disputes of workmen in Contract 


1990/SRAVANA 20, 1912 [Part II— Sfc. 3001 

i^acour (Regulation & Abolition) Act, 1970 imply 
uiat li me worxmen are not htred througn mo 
contractor noiding valid licence under the Act tie 
would be a woiTsman employed by the management 
itseil. 

Ill me picsL. L Case the management has uedher 
piuauecd rcgisiration ot its estaolisUraent nor nas 
jiS piouuceu me eomract licence, unaer me Contract 
i^aoour ticeguiation Ac. Abolitionj Act. liie neces¬ 
sary imp icaiion is that tUe concerned workmen 
.siiouin oe considered lo me worltmen ot tne prmci- 
pai employer i.e. management ot Bniirungia FrojeCi. 

21. Sluri G. Prasad, Advocate for the manage¬ 
ment, Jias contended mat me Contract Caoour 
ticegulanon & Abolition) Act provides lor penal 
acuon for breach of me provisions of me Act, 
/according to inm, me concerned, workmen cannot 
oe regaraed as the workmen of the principal em¬ 
ployer simply because the provisions of die Act 
nave been violated. 

Indeed, the penal provisions are provided in the 
Act, to dissuade employer from attempts to com¬ 
mit breach of the provisions of me Act and Rules 
made thereunder. But they do not detract from the 
position that tlicre can be no deemed contract lab¬ 
our if the two conditions, namely, registration of 
esiablishraent by the principal employer and licence 
of the contractors are not satisfied. Hence, I have 
no hesitation to over-rule the contention of Shti 
Prasad and hold that the concerned workmen are 
really the workmen of the principal employer i.e. 
ithe management of Bhurnngia Project because the 
principal employer has failed to produce registration 
of its establishment and licence for the contractors 
to operate under Contract Labour (Regulation & 
Abolition) Act. 

22. In the present reference I have been called 
upon to adjudicate if the action of the manage¬ 
ment in not regularising the concerned workmen 
and not paying them N. C. W. A. II wages is 
justified or not. 

Admittedly, the concerned workmen have not 
been regularised in service of Dhurungia Project. 
When they are considered to be the employees of 
the principal employer i.e. the management of the 
Project regularisation of their services under the 
management is a fait accompli because the em¬ 
ployees cannot be allowed to suffer by working 
with wages lower than the scale of wages as per 
service bondition nor will the employer be allowed 
to adopt any subterfuse to avoid or range the ser¬ 
vice condition by offering a dfferent pay or pay 
scale. This being the position, the action of the 
management of the Project in not regularising' the 
services of the concerned workmen is not. consi¬ 
dered to be justified and the action of the man¬ 
agement is not offering them scale of pay as avail¬ 
able under N. C. W- As, particularly N. O. W. A. 
II is not also justified. I consider that the con¬ 
cerned workmen should be regularised in service 
and should be allowed regular scale of pay as per 
N. C. W. As nrid 50 of back wages exclusive of 
wages already paid to them.

23. Accordingly, the following award is ren¬ 
dered—^thc action of the management of Bhnrungia 
Project of Mjs. Bharat Coking Coal Limited, Post 
Office Mohnda, Dist. Dhanbad, in not regularising 
the concerned v'orkman and not paying them the 
.scale of wages as per N, C. W, As. particularly 
N. C. W. A. 11 is not Justified. The management is 
directed to regularise the concerned workmen in 
service, to pay them wages as per N. C. W. As. 
particularly N. C, W. A. 11 and 50 of back wages 
exclusive of wages already paid to them with effect 
ffom the date of the present reference i.e I4th,

In the circuirsiar.ces of the i.ase, I award no 
cost.
